Chern Classes of Open Projected Richardson Varieties and of Affine Schubert Cells
Neil J.Y. Fan, Peter L. Guo, Changjian Su, Rui Xiong

Abstract
. The open projected Richardson varieties form a stratification for the partial flag variety G / P G/P . We compare the Segre–MacPherson classes of open projected Richardson varieties with those of the corresponding affine Schubert cells by pushing or pulling them to the affine Grassmannian. In the Grassmannian case, the open projected Richardson varieties are well known as open positroid varieties. We obtain symmetric functions that represent the Segre–MacPherson classes of open positroid varieties, constructed explicitly in terms of pipe dreams for affine permutations.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
14M15, 14C17, 05E10

1.
Introduction

Let
G
be a reductive group such that the derived subgroup
G^{\prime}
is simple, and let
B
and
B^{-}
be the Borel and opposite Borel subgroups,
T=B\cap B^{-}
the maximal torus, and
W
the associated Weyl group.
For
u\leq w\in W
in the Bruhat order, the
open Richardson variety

\mathring{R}_{u,w}
over the full flag variety
G/B
is the intersection of the Schubert cell
\mathring{\Sigma}_{w}=BwB/B
and the opposite Schubert cell
\mathring{\Sigma}^{u}=B^{-}uB/B
, whose closure is the
closed Richardson variety

R_{u,w}
.

Fix a parabolic subgroup
P
containing
B
. Let
\pi\colon G/B\rightarrow G/P
be the natural projection. The
open projected Richardson variety
is
\mathring{\Pi}_{u,w}:=\pi(\mathring{R}_{u,w})
. Its closure
\Pi_{u,w}:=\pi(R_{u,w})
is the
closed projected Richardson variety
, which originates from the study of total positivity and Poisson geometry, see for example
[
30
,
38
,
13
]
.
Let
W_{P}
be the Weyl group of
P
, and
W^{P}
the minimal length coset representatives of
W/W_{P}
. Knutson, Lam and Speyer
[
20
]
showed that the open projected Richardson varieties
\mathring{\Pi}_{u,w}
, where
w
ranges over elements in
W^{P}
(or equivalently, over equivalence classes of
P
-Bruhat intervals
[
20
, Section 2]
), form a stratification of
G/P
.
Many of the geometric properties of Richardson varieties were shown to hold for projected Richardson varieties
[
20
]
, see also Billey and Coskun
[
4
]
.

The projected Richardson varieties over Grassmannians are known as
positroid varieties
studied systematically by Knutson, Lam and Speyer
[
19
]
, motivated by previous work of Postnikov
[
37
]
.
Positroid varieties have drawn increasing attention in combinatorics, representation theory, and algebraic geometry. For example, they are related to affine Grassmannian
[
15
]
,
Gromov–Witten invariants
[
7
,
6
]
, cluster algebras
[
11
]
, knot invariants
[
12
]
, and we refer the readers to the excellent surveys
[
26
,
43
]
.

In this paper, we investigate the Chern–Schwartz–MacPherson (CSM) and Segre–MacPherson (SM) classes of open projected Richardson varieties.
These classes are generalizations of the usual Chern classes of smooth varieties to singular varieties
X
, see for example
[
31
,
40
,
41
,
35
]
.
They are assigned to constructible functions on the variety, and behave well under pushforwards.
We will focus on the characteristic function
\mathbbm{1}_{Y}
of certain locally closed subvariety
Y
inside
X
.
When
X=G/B
, the CSM classes of Schubert cells
\mathring{\Sigma}_{w}
are equivalent to the Maulik–Okounkov stable envelopes, thereby having close connections with the representation theory of the group
G
, see
[
2
,
32
]
.

On the other hand, to the group
G
, we have the affine flag variety
\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
and the affine Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}_{G}
, which are both infinite dimensional variants of the finite flag variety. They play a crucial role in the geometric representation theory
[
44
]
, and draw special interests because of their relation to quantum Schubert calculus
[
36
,
29
,
18
]
.
For an element
f
in the extended affine Weyl group
\widehat{W}
, the
affine Schubert cell

\mathring{\Sigma}_{f}\subset\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
is finite-dimensional.
So its CSM class
c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{f})
is well defined in the (small) torus equivariant homology group
H^{T}_{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
.
These classes form a basis for the localized equivariant homology group. Inspired by the behavior of CSM and SM classes over the finite type flag varieties
[
2
]
, we define the SM classes
s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})
of the
opposite affine Schubert cells

\mathring{\Sigma}^{f}
to be the dual basis of the CSM classes, similar to the Schubert classes considered by Kostant and Kumar
[
22
]
.

Recall the affine Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}_{G}=G(\!(z)\!)/G[[z]]
.
Let us fix a dominant cocharacter
\lambda
, such that the stabilizer subgroup
W_{\lambda}
equals the parabolic subgroup
W_{P}
.
Then the
G
-orbit of the element
z^{-\lambda}G[[z]]/G[[z]]\in\operatorname{Gr}_{G}
is isomorphic to
G/P
, and its
G[[z]]
-orbit
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}
, the spherical Schubert variety, is an affine bundle over the partial flag variety
G/P
.
Let us consider the following diagram,

\textstyle{G/P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Gr}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Fl}_{G},}

where
i_{\lambda}
and
j_{\lambda}
are inclusions, and
r
is a continuous section of the projection
r:\operatorname{Fl}_{G}\to\operatorname{Gr}_{G}
defined as follows.
Let
K\subset G
be the maximal compact subgroup and
T_{\mathbb{R}}:=K\cap T
the compact torus. Then
r
is the continuous map

r\colon\operatorname{Gr}_{G}\simeq\Omega K\rightarrow LK\rightarrow LK/T_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\operatorname{Fl}_{G},

where
LK
(resp.,
\Omega K
) is the space of polynomial maps
S^{1}\to K
(resp.
(S^{1},1)\to(K,1)
). Therefore, we can pushforward
i_{\lambda,*}
classes on
G/P
to
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}
, and also pullback
j_{\lambda}^{*}\circ r^{*}
classes on
\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
to
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}
.

Our first main result establishes a relationship between the SM classes of open projected Richardson varieties and the SM classes of opposite affine Schubert cells in the affine flag variety.

Theorem A

(Theorem
6.1
)
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be the normal bundle of
G/P
inside
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}
. Then, for
u\leq w
with
w\in W^{P}
,

i_{\lambda,*}\bigg(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{u,w})\cdot c^{T}(\mathcal{N})^{-1}\bigg)=(j_{\lambda}^{*}\circ r^{*})\bigg(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})\bigg)\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda})_{\operatorname{loc}},

where
f=ut_{\lambda}w^{-1}
is an element in the extended affine Weyl group
\widehat{W}
,
and
c^{T}(\mathcal{N})
is the
T
-equivariant total Chern class of
\mathcal{N}
.

Taking the lowest degree terms on both sides leads to a connection concerning the cohomology classes of projected Richardson varieties and affine Schubert varieties studied by He and Lam
[
15
, Theorem 5.8]
, which, if further restricting to the type
A
Grassmannian case, recovers
[
19
, Theorem 7.8]
.
Indeed, the above implication is one of our main motivations of this work. It is also worth mentioning that our proof differs from that used in
[
15
]
and seems simpler. The proof in
loc. cit.
depends heavily on the Billey-type localization formulae for Schubert classes.
Instead, we employ the left and right Demazure–Lusztig operators to deduce that both sides satisfy the same recurrences, which uniquely determine these classes. We believe that this strategy should still work in the equivariant
K
-theory setting.

We next switch to the combinatorial sides of this paper. The study of characteristic functions of open Richardson varieties and their CSM classes comes with the
extended
P
-Bruhat order

\leq_{P}
on
W
. This is exhibited in Theorem
3.12
:

\mathsf{Fun}(G/P)\ni\pi_{*}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathring{R}_{u,w}})\neq 0\iff u\leq_{P}w.

As a comparison, it was shown in

[
20
, Lemma 3.1 and Corollary 3.4]
that

H^{*}(G/P)\ni\pi_{*}([R_{u,w}])\neq 0\iff u\leq_{P}^{\prime}w,

where
\leq_{P}^{\prime}
is the ordinary
P
-Bruhat order.
A new feature of the extended
P
-Bruhat order is the
W_{P}
-invariance property.
In fact, Theorem
3.6
implies that it is the strongest
W_{P}
-invariant partial order on
W
weaker than the Bruhat order.

When restricting to the Grassmannian
G/P=\operatorname{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
, as aforementioned, (open) projective Richardson varieties are known as (open) positroid varieties, which are indexed by bounded affine permutations
f\in\tilde{S}_{n}
by
[
19
]
. As our another main result, we explicitly obtain a symmetric rational function representative
\tilde{F}_{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k};y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
, which is symmetric in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
, for the SM class
of the open positroid variety
\mathring{\Pi}_{f}
.

Theorem B

(Theorem
7.7
)
.

For a bounded affine permutation
f
, we have

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})=\tilde{F}_{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k};y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{C}^{n}))_{\operatorname{loc}},

where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
are the Chern roots of the dual of the tautological bundle over
\operatorname{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
, and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt})
are the standard equivariant parameters.

The function
\tilde{F}_{f}
is constructed via a weighted counting of certain colored string diagrams, which obviously admit a pipe dream realization.
The proof of Theorem
B
relies on a localization formula for SM classes along with a diagrammatic calculus.

If we focus on the lowest degree terms on both sides of the formula in Theorem
B
, then by comparing with a recent work (in progress) of Shimozono and Zhang
[
42
]
, the right-hand side will exactly be
the double affine Stanley symmetric function in
[
28
]
.
Therefore, if further letting
y_{1}=\cdots=y_{n}=0
, the right-hand side becomes the ordinary
affine Stanley symmetric function of Lam
[
25
]
, and thus in this case Theorem
B
specializes to
[
19
, Theorem 7.1]
.

This paper is organized as follows. Section
2
is devoted to the geometric background. In Section
3
, we define the extended
P
-Bruhat order and give several equivalent characterizations.
Section
4
and Section
5
provide recursions satisfied by the finite and affine Chern classes.
Section
6
finishes the proof of Theorem
A
. Finally, in Section
7
, our task is to prove Theorem

B
. This is achieved by combining a localization formula for SM classes with diagrammatic computations on string diagrams.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Segre classes and Chern classes

Let
X
be a complex algebraic variety. The group of constructible functions
\mathsf{Fun}(X)
consists of functions
\varphi=\sum_{W}c_{W}\mathbbm{1}_{W}
, where the sum is over a finite set of constructible subsets
W\subset X
,
c_{W}\in\mathbb{Z}
are integers, and
\mathbbm{1}_{W}
is the characteristic function of
W
. For a proper morphism
f\colon Y\rightarrow X
, there is a linear map
f_{*}\colon\mathsf{Fun}(Y)\rightarrow\mathsf{Fun}(X)
, such that for any constructible subset
W\subset Y
,

(1)

f_{*}(\mathbbm{1}_{W})(x)=\chi_{\rm top}(f^{-1}(x)\cap W),\

where
x\in X
and
\chi_{\text{top}}
denotes the topological Euler characteristic.
Thus,
\mathsf{Fun}
can be considered as a (covariant) functor from the category of complex algebraic varieties and proper morphisms to the category of abelian groups.

According to a conjecture attributed to Deligne and Grothendieck (see
[
14
, Note 87
1
]
), there is a unique natural transformation
c_{*}\colon\mathsf{Fun}\to H_{*}
from the functor of constructible functions on a complex algebraic variety
X
to the Borel–Moore homology functor, where all morphisms are proper, such that if
X
is smooth then
c_{*}(\mathbbm{1}_{X})=c(TX)\cap[X]
, where
c(TX)
denotes the total Chern class of the tangent bundle
TX
and
[X]
denotes the fundamental class. This conjecture was proved by MacPherson
[
31
]
; the class

c_{*}(\mathbbm{1}_{X})
for possibly singular
X
was shown to coincide with a class defined earlier by Schwartz
[
40
,
41
,
8
]
.

The theory of CSM classes was later extended to the equivariant setting by Ohmoto
[
35
]
. If
X
has an
action of a torus
T
, Ohmoto defined the group
\mathsf{Fun}^{T}(X)
of
equivariant
constructible functions. Ohmoto
[
35
, Theorem 1.1]
proves that there is an equivariant version of MacPherson transformation
c_{*}^{T}\colon\mathsf{Fun}^{T}(X)\to H_{*}^{T}(X)
that satisfies
c_{*}^{T}(\mathbbm{1}_{X})=c^{T}(TX)\cap[X]_{T}
if
X
is a non-singular variety, and that is functorial with respect to proper push-forwards. The last statement means that for all proper
T
-equivariant morphisms
Y\to X
the following diagram
commutes:

\textstyle{\mathsf{Fun}^{T}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{*}^{T}}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}^{T}}
\textstyle{H_{*}^{T}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}^{T}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Fun}^{T}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{*}^{T}}
\textstyle{H_{*}^{T}(X).}

If
X
is smooth, we will identify the (equivariant) homology and cohomology groups by Poincaré duality:
H_{*}^{T}(X)\simeq H^{*}_{T}(X)
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
Z
be a
T
-invariant constructible subvariety of
X
.

(1)

We denote by
c_{\rm SM}(Z):=c_{*}^{T}(\mathbbm{1}_{Z}){~\in H_{*}^{T}(X)}
the equivariant Chern–Schwartz–MacPherson (CSM) class of
Z
.

(2)

If
X
is smooth, we denote by
s_{\rm SM}(Z):=\frac{c_{*}^{T}(\mathbbm{1}_{Z})}{c^{T}(TX)}~\in\widehat{H}^{*}_{T}(X)

the equivariant Segre–MacPherson (SM) class of
Z
, where
\widehat{H}^{*}_{T}(X)
is an appropriate completion
of
H^{*}_{T}(X)
.

2.2.
Affine Flag Varieties

Let
X_{*}(T)
be the cocharacter lattice of
T
, and let

\widehat{W}:=W\ltimes X_{*}(T)

be the
extended affine Weyl group
.
For a cocharacter
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)
, we denote by
t_{\lambda}
the corresponding element in
\widehat{W}
. Note that in
\widehat{W}
, we have

wt_{\lambda}w^{-1}=t_{w\lambda},\qquad w\in W,\lambda\in X_{*}(T).

Denote the coroot lattice as
Q^{\vee}\subseteq X_{*}(T)
.
It is known that the subgroup
W_{a}:=W\ltimes Q^{\vee}
, called the
affine Weyl group
, is the Coxeter group of the corresponding affine Dynkin diagram
[
17
]
with generators

s_{i}\in W\,(i\in I),\quad\text{ and \quad}s_{0}=t_{\theta^{\vee}}s_{\theta},

where
\theta
is the highest root.
Let
R
be the associated root system of
W
, and
R^{+}
(resp.,
R^{-}
) be the set of positive roots
\alpha>0
(resp., negative roots
\alpha<0
).
The length function on
W_{a}
can be extended to
\widehat{W}
, which owns the Iwahori–Matsumoto formula
[
16
]
:

\ell(wt_{\lambda})=\sum_{\alpha>0,w\alpha>0}\big|\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle\big|+\sum_{\alpha>0,w\alpha<0}\big|\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle+1\big|.

Let
\mathbb{C}[[z]]
(resp.
\mathbb{C}(\!(z)\!):=\mathbb{C}[[z]][z^{-1}]
) be the formal power series ring (resp. formal Laurent series ring), and let
G[[z]]
(resp.
G(\!(z)\!)
) be the
\mathbb{C}[[z]]
-points (resp.
\mathbb{C}(\!(z)\!)
-points) of
G
. There is an evaluation at
z=0
map from
G[[z]]
to
G
, and let
\mathcal{I}
be the inverse image of the Borel subgroup
B
. Then the
affine flag variety
is

\operatorname{Fl}_{G}=G(\!(z)\!)/\mathcal{I},

whose
T
-fixed points

(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})^{T}
can be identified with
\widehat{W}
as follows. Any cocharacter
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)
defines a morphism
\mathbb{C}(\!(z)\!)^{*}\rightarrow T(\!(z)\!)\subset G(\!(z)\!)
, we use
z^{\lambda}\in G(\!(z)\!)
to denote the image of
z
. For any
w\in W
, let
\dot{w}
denote a lift of it in
G[[z]]
. Then for each
wt_{\lambda}\in\widehat{W}
, the corresponding fixed point in
\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
is
\dot{w}z^{-\lambda}\mathcal{I}\in\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
, which will be just denoted by
wt_{\lambda}
throughout the paper. Let
\mathring{\Sigma}_{wt_{\lambda}}:=\mathcal{I}\dot{w}z^{-\lambda}\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}
be the
affine Schubert cell
of dimension
\ell(wt_{\lambda})
. The affine flag variety has a cell decomposition

\operatorname{Fl}_{G}=\bigsqcup_{wt_{\lambda}\in\widehat{W}}\mathring{\Sigma}_{wt_{\lambda}}.

For each
n\geq 0
, let

X_{n}:=\bigsqcup_{wt_{\lambda}\in\widehat{W},\,\ell(wt_{\lambda})\leq n}\mathring{\Sigma}_{wt_{\lambda}}.

Then
\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
is the increasing limit of
X_{n}
, which is called an ind-variety.

There is a loop rotation action of
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\operatorname{rot}}:=\mathbb{C}^{*}
on
\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
by scaling the parameter
z
. Let
\delta
be the degree one character of this action, which is also the imaginary root for the corresponding Kac–Moody Lie algebra
\hat{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then the positive (real) affine roots are

(2)

R^{+}_{\operatorname{aff}}=\{\alpha+k\delta\mid\alpha\in R^{+},k\geq 0\text{ or }\alpha\in R^{-},k\geq 1\},

and the negative (real) affine roots are

R^{-}_{\operatorname{aff}}=\{\alpha+k\delta\mid\alpha\in R^{+},k\leq-1\text{ or }\alpha\in R^{-},k\leq 0\}.

The extended affine Weyl group
\widehat{W}
acts on the lattice
X^{*}(T)\oplus\mathbbm{Z}\delta
by the following formula (see
[
29
, (3.1)]
)

(3)

wt_{\lambda}(\mu+k\delta)=w(\mu)+(k-\langle{\lambda,\mu\rangle})\delta,

where
\mu\in X^{*}(T)
.

Lemma 2.2
.

For any dominant cocharacter
\lambda
, the torus
T\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\operatorname{rot}}
weights of the tangent space
T_{t_{\lambda}}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{t_{\lambda}})
are

\{\alpha+k\delta\mid\alpha\in R^{-},1\leq k\leq-\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle\}.

Proof.

Since
\mathring{\Sigma}_{t_{\lambda}}\simeq\mathcal{I}/(\mathcal{I}\cap z^{-\lambda}\mathcal{I}z^{\lambda})
, the desired weights are

R_{\operatorname{aff}}^{+}\setminus(R_{\operatorname{aff}}^{+}\cap\operatorname{Ad}_{t_{\lambda}}(R_{\operatorname{aff}}^{+})).

By (
2
) and (
3
),

R_{\operatorname{aff}}^{+}\cap\operatorname{Ad}_{t_{\lambda}}(R_{\operatorname{aff}}^{+})=\{\alpha+k\delta\mid\alpha\in R^{+},k\geq 0,\text{ or }\alpha\in R^{-},k\geq 1-\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle\}.

Hence, the lemma holds.
∎

Let
\operatorname{Gr}_{G}:=G(\!(z)\!)/G[[z]]
be the
affine Grassmannian
. There is a natural projection map
\operatorname{Fl}_{G}\rightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{G}
whose fibers are isomorphic to
G/B
. The
T
-fixed points
(\operatorname{Gr}_{G})^{T}
are in bijection with the coset
\widehat{W}/W\simeq X_{*}(T)
. For any
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)
, we let
t_{\lambda}W
represent the fixed point
z^{-\lambda}G[[z]]/G[[z]]
. For any dominant cocharacter
\lambda
, let
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}:=G[[z]]z^{-\lambda}G[[z]]/G[[z]]\subset\operatorname{Gr}_{G}
. Then

\operatorname{Gr}_{G}=\bigsqcup_{\lambda\in X_{*}(T)^{+}}\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}.

Let
\operatorname{ev}:G[[z]]\rightarrow G
be the evaluation at
z=0
map. Recall that the parabolic subgroup
P
containing the positive Borel subgroup
B
is associated with the simple roots

\{\alpha_{i}\mid\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\rangle=0\}
. By (
3
),

\operatorname{ev}\bigg(G[[z]]\cap z^{-\lambda}G[[z]]z^{\lambda}\bigg)=P\subset G.

Hence,

\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\simeq G[[z]]/(G[[z]]\cap z^{-\lambda}G[[z]]z^{\lambda})

maps to
G/P
via the map
\operatorname{ev}
. Moreover, it
is a bundle of affine spaces over
G/P
,
and there is a closed embedding

i_{\lambda}:G/P\simeq G\cdot z^{-\lambda}G[[z]]/G[[z]]\subset\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda},

by regarding
G
as a subgroup of
G[[z]]
of constant loops, see
[
44
]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

The
T\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\operatorname{rot}}
weights of the tangent space
T_{t_{\lambda}W}\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}
is

\{-\alpha+k\delta\mid 0\leq k<\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle\}.

Proof.

By
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}=G[[z]]/(G[[z]]\cap z^{-\lambda}G[[z]]z^{\lambda})
, and (
3
),
the desired weights are

\displaystyle\quad\{\alpha+k\delta\mid\alpha\in R,k\geq 0\}\setminus\{\alpha+k\delta\mid\alpha\in R^{+},k\geq 0,\text{ or }\alpha\in R^{-},k\geq-\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle\}

\displaystyle=\{\alpha+k\delta\mid\alpha\in R^{-},0\leq k<-\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle\}

\displaystyle=\{-\alpha+k\delta\mid\alpha\in R^{+},0\leq k<\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle\}.\qed

3.
Extended
P
-Bruhat order

In this section, we define the extended
P
-Bruhat order on a Weyl group and give several characterizations for this order.

3.1.
Extended
P
-Bruhat order

For a parabolic subgroup
W_{P}
of
W
, denote by
R^{+}_{P}
the associated positive system of
W_{P}
.
For
u,w\in W
, write
u\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}w
to mean that
w=us_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in R^{+}\setminus R^{+}_{P}
such that
\ell(w)>\ell(v)
.
Notice that the condition
\ell(w)>\ell(v)
is equivalent to saying that
u(\alpha)\in R^{+}
.
The
extended
P
-Bruhat order

\leq_{P}
on
W
is the transitive closure of the relations
u\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}w
,
that is,

u\leq_{P}w\iff\text{there exists a path }u=u_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}u_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}u_{k-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}u_{k}=w.

Remark 3.1
.

In the definition of
u\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}w
, if replacing the condition
\ell(w)>\ell(u)
by
\ell(w)=\ell(u)+1
, then the transitive closure forms the ordinary
P
-Bruhat order as introduced in
[
20
]
. To distinguish, we shall denote the ordinary
P
-Bruhat order by
\leq^{\prime}_{P}
.
It is clear that
u\leq^{\prime}_{P}w
implies
u\leq_{P}w
.

Example 3.2
.

Let us consider the rank
2
case.
Then

W=\langle s,t|s^{2}=t^{2}=(st)^{m}=1\rangle,\qquad W_{P}=\langle s\rangle\subset W,

where
m=3,4
, or
6
, i.e.,
W
is of type
A_{2},B_{2}=C_{2},G_{2}
,
see Figure

Example 3.3
.

The case
W=S_{4}
and
W_{P}=S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{1}
is illustrated in Figure
2
.

Figure 2.
W=S_{4}
and
W_{P}=S_{1}\times S_{2}\times S_{1}

Example 3.4
.

When
W=S_{n}
is the Weyl group of type
A_{n-1}
and
W_{P}=S_{k}\times S_{n-k}
is a maximal parabolic subgroup, we have

\{s_{\alpha}:\alpha\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+}\}=\{\tau_{ab}:1\leq a\leq k<b\leq n\},

where
\tau_{ab}\in S_{n}
is the tranposition of
a
and
b
.
In this case, the extended
P
-Bruhat order is the
extended
k
-Bruhat order
\leq_{k}
investigated in
[
9
, Lemma 6.4]
, which has the following combinatorial description:

u\leq_{k}w\iff\begin{cases}\forall\,1\leq a\leq k,\,u(a)\leq w(a),\\
\forall\,k<b\leq n,\,u(b)\geq w(b).\end{cases}

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
u,w\in W
.

(1)

For
v\in W_{P}
, we have

u\leq_{P}w\iff uv\leq_{P}wv
.

(2)

For
w\in W^{P}
, we have

u\leq_{P}w\iff u\leq w
.

Proof.

(
1
)
Clearly, we only need to check that
u\leq_{P}w\,\Longrightarrow\,uv\leq_{P}wv
for any
v\in W_{P}
. It suffices to verify
u\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}w\,\Longrightarrow\,uv\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}wv
.
Assume that

w=us_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+}
with
u(\alpha)\in R^{+}
.
Notice that

wv=us_{\alpha}v=uvv^{-1}s_{\alpha}v=uvs_{v^{-1}(\alpha)}.

Since
v\in W_{P}
and
\alpha\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+}
, we have
v^{-1}(\alpha)\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+}
.
This, together with the fact that
uv(v^{-1}(\alpha))=u(\alpha)\in R^{+}
, implies
uv\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\to}}wv
.

(
2
)
For any
w\in W
, it is obvious that

u\leq_{P}w\Longrightarrow u\leq w.
We check the reverse direction. Suppose that
u\leq w
with
w\in W^{P}
. Then
u\leq^{\prime}_{P}w
in the ordinary
P
-Bruhat order
[
20
, Proposition 2.5]
. This yields that
u\leq_{P}w
.
∎

Combining the above gives the following equivalent statements.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
u,w\in W
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

u\leq_{P}w
;

(2)

uv\leq_{P}wv
for any
v\in W_{P}
;

(3)

uv\leq wv
for some
v\in W_{P}
such that
wv\in W^{P}
.

Example 3.7
.

Recall that the Coxeter group of type
BC_{n}
can be realized as

W=\{w\in S_{\{\pm 1,\ldots,\pm n\}}:w(-i)=-w(i)\}.

We consider the parabolic subgroup
W_{P}\cong S_{n}
of
w\in W
such that
w(i)>0
for
i>0
. Then we have

(4)

u\leq_{P}w\iff\forall 1\leq i\leq n,\,u(i)\geq w(i).

To see this, we consider another Weyl group
\mathcal{W}=S_{2n}
with parabolic subgroup
\mathcal{W}_{P}=S_{n}\times S_{n}
.
We have a natural embedding

i:W\subset\mathcal{W}
such that

u\leq w\iff i(u)\leq i(w),

see
[
5
, Section 8.1]
.
Moreover, it is direct to check that

w\in W_{P}\iff i(w)\in\mathcal{W}_{P},\qquad w\in W^{P}\iff i(w)\in\mathcal{W}^{P}.

By Theorem
3.6
, we have

u\leq_{P}w\iff i(u)\leq_{P}i(w).

By Example
3.4
, we get (
4
).

3.2.
Affine characterization

Let us fix a dominant cocharacter
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)
.
For
u,w\in W
, we define

(5)

f_{u,w}^{\lambda}=ut_{\lambda}w^{-1}\in\widehat{W}.

Assume that the stabilizer of
\lambda
in
W
is the parabolic subgroup
W_{P}
. Let
W\lambda
be the Weyl group orbit of
\lambda
.

Theorem 3.8
.

For
u,w\in W
, we have

u\leq_{P}w\iff f_{u,w}^{\lambda}\leq t_{\mu}\text{ for some $\mu\in W\lambda$}.

To give a proof of Theorem
3.8
, we need the following length formula.

Lemma 3.9
.

If
w\in W^{P}
, then

\ell(f^{\lambda}_{u,w})=\ell(u)+\ell(t_{\lambda})-\ell(w).

Proof.

It is known
[
29
, Lemma 3.3]
that for
w\in W^{P}
,
t_{\lambda}w^{-1}
is the minimal representative of the right coset
Wt_{\lambda}w^{-1}
.
So

(6)

\ell(f^{\lambda}_{u,w})=\ell(ut_{\lambda}w^{-1})=\ell(u)+\ell(t_{\lambda}w^{-1}).

Since

\ell(f^{\lambda}_{w,w})=\ell(t_{w\lambda})=\ell(t_{\lambda})=\ell(f^{\lambda}_{\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id}})=\sum_{\alpha\in R^{+}}|\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle|,

we obtain that

\ell(w)+\ell(t_{\lambda}w^{-1})=\ell(t_{\lambda})
,
which, together with (
6
), leads to the desired formula in the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.8
.

Notice that when
v\in W_{P}
,

f^{\lambda}_{uv,wv}=uvt_{\lambda}v^{-1}w=ut_{\lambda}w^{-1}=f^{\lambda}_{u,w}.

Hence, according to (1) in Proposition
3.5
, we may assume
w\in W^{P}
. In this situation, by (2) in Proposition
3.5
,
u\leq_{P}w
is equivalent to
u\leq w
.
If
u\leq w
, by Lemma
3.9
, the decomposition
f^{\lambda}_{u,w}=u\cdot t_{\lambda}w^{-1}
is reduced. So

f^{\lambda}_{u,w}=ut_{\lambda}w^{-1}\leq wt_{\lambda}w^{-1}=t_{w\lambda}.

Conversely, assume that
\mu=w_{+}\lambda
for some
w_{+}\in W^{P}
.
Then

f_{u,w}^{\lambda}=ut_{\lambda}w^{-1}\leq t_{\mu}=w_{+}t_{\lambda}w_{+}^{-1}=f^{\lambda}_{w^{+},w^{+}}.

By
[
15
, Proposition 2.1]
1
1

1

Note that
t^{-\lambda}
in
loc. cit.
is denoted by
t_{\lambda}
here.
, there exists
v\in W_{P}
such that

u\leq w_{+}v,\qquad w\geq w_{+}v.

So we have
u\leq w
.
∎

3.3.
Geometric characterization

Recall that for a constructible subset
Y
of a complex algebraic variety
X
,

\mathbbm{1}_{Y}\in\mathsf{Fun}(X)
denotes its characteristic function.

Lemma 3.10
.

For any
v\in W_{P}
and
u,w\in W
, we have

\pi_{*}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathring{R}_{u,w}})=\pi_{*}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathring{R}_{uv,wv}}).

Remark 3.11
.

From the proof below, we see that the equality
\pi(\mathring{R}_{u,w})=\pi(\mathring{R}_{uv,wv})
does not hold in general.

Proof of Lemma
3.10
.

First of all, we can assume
w\in W^{P}
.
It suffices to show when
v=s_{i}
is a simple reflection in
W_{P}
.
Now the projection
\pi
factorizes into

G/B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G/P_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}G/P

for
P_{i}=B\cup Bs_{i}B
the minimal parabolic subgroup corresponding to
i\in I
. The Lemma follows if
\pi_{i*}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathring{R}_{u,w}})=\pi_{i*}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathring{R}_{us_{i},ws_{i}}})
. Therefore, the
Lemma is further reduced to the case when
P=P_{i}
and
\pi=\pi_{i}
. Since
w\in W^{P}
, the case of
u\in W^{P}
is proved in
[
20
, Lemma 3.1]
.

Now we prove the other case
us_{i}>u
. Note that
\pi_{i}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
bundle.
At any point
z\in G/P_{i}
, we need to show

\chi\big(\mathring{R}_{u,w}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)\big)=\chi\big(\mathring{R}_{us_{i},ws_{i}}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)\big).

The intersection of the Schubert cells
\mathring{\Sigma}_{w}
,
\mathring{\Sigma}_{ws_{i}}
and the fibre
\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
has two possibilities

(1)

\mathring{\Sigma}_{w}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)=\{p\}
a point,
and
\mathring{\Sigma}_{ws_{i}}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)=\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)\setminus\{p\}
an affine line;

(2)

both intersections are empty.

Similarly, the intersection of the opposite Schubert cells
\mathring{\Sigma}^{u}
,
\mathring{\Sigma}^{us_{i}}
and the fibre
\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
has two possible possibilities

(a)

\mathring{\Sigma}^{u}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)=\{q\}
a point, and
\mathring{\Sigma}^{us_{i}}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)=\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)\setminus\{q\}
an affine line;

(b)

both intersections are empty.

In the case (2) or (b), we have

\mathring{R}_{u,w}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)=\mathring{R}_{us_{i},ws_{i}}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)=\varnothing.

Hence, we only need to deal with case (1) and (a). When
p=q
, we have

\mathring{R}_{u,w}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)=\{p\},\qquad\mathring{R}_{us_{i},ws_{i}}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)=\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)\setminus\{p\}\simeq\mathbb{C};

when
p\neq q
, we have

\mathring{R}_{u,w}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)=\varnothing,\qquad\mathring{R}_{us_{i},ws_{i}}\cap\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)=\pi_{i}^{-1}(z)\setminus\{p,q\}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

In both cases, they have the same Euler characteristics.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 3.12
.

For
u,w\in W
, we have

\pi_{*}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathring{R}_{u,w}})\neq 0\iff u\leq_{P}w.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.10
and Proposition
3.5
, we can assume
w\in W^{P}
.
In this case, the restriction of
\pi
to
\mathring{\Sigma}_{w}
is injective, thus
\pi_{*}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathring{R}_{u,w}})\neq 0
if and only if
\mathring{R}_{u,w}\neq\varnothing
, i.e.
u\leq w
.
Hence, the theorem follows from Theorem
3.6
.
∎

4.
Recursion of Chern classes, finite part

In this section, we will characterize the CSM classes of open projected Richardson varieties.

4.1.
Chern classes of open Richardson varieties

Let us first characterize the CSM class of (unprojected) open Richardson varieties.
Firstly, since the Schubert cells
\mathring{\Sigma}_{w}
and
\mathring{\Sigma}^{u}
intersect transversally, we have (see
[
39
]
)

(7)

c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})=c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{w})\cdot s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{u}).

Let us recall some operators acting on
H_{T}^{*}(G/B)
from
[
33
]
. The group
G
acts on
G/B
by left multiplication. Hence, we have a Weyl group action on
H_{T}^{*}(G/B)
, which is denoted by
w^{L}
for any
w\in W
.

Define

\delta_{i}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}(\operatorname{id}-s_{i}^{L}),\qquad T_{i}^{L}=s_{i}^{L}-\delta_{i},\qquad T_{i}^{L,\vee}=s_{i}^{L}+\delta_{i},

i.e.,

(8)

T_{i}^{L}=\frac{-1}{\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{id}+\frac{\alpha_{i}+1}{\alpha_{i}}s_{i}^{L},\qquad T_{i}^{L,\vee}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{id}+\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{\alpha_{i}}s_{i}^{L}.

Then we have (
[
33
, Theorem 4.3]
)

(9)

T_{i}^{L}(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{w}))=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{s_{i}w}),\qquad T_{i}^{L,\vee}(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{u}))=c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{s_{i}u}).

For the CSM classes of the Richardson cells, we have the following recursion formula.

Theorem 4.1
.

For
u,w\in W
and
i\in I
, we have

(10)

\displaystyle s_{i}^{L}(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w}))+\alpha_{i}\cdot s_{i}^{L}(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{s_{i}u,w}))=c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})+\alpha_{i}\cdot c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,s_{i}w}),

where whenever
u\not\leq w
, the summand
c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})
is understood to be zero.

Proof.

Let
\varphi,\psi\in H_{T}^{*}(G/B)
. From the definition of
T_{i}^{L}
and
T_{i}^{L,\vee}
, we have

s_{i}^{L}(\varphi\cdot\psi)+\alpha_{i}\cdot s_{i}^{L}(\varphi\cdot T_{i}^{L,\vee}(\psi))=\varphi\cdot\psi+\alpha_{i}\cdot(T_{i}^{L}(\varphi)\cdot\psi).

Applying this formula to
\varphi=c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{w})
,
\psi=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{u})
, and using (
7
) and (
9
), we get the desired formula.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

Using the right Demazure–Lusztig operators from
[
33
]
, it is possible to prove another recursion for
c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})
via the same method.

4.2.
Chern classes of open projected Richardson varieties

Let
P
be a parabolic subgroup of
G
containing
B
. Recall that
\pi:G/B\rightarrow G/P
is the natural projection. For any
u\leq w\in W^{P}
, the open projected Richardson variety is
\mathring{\Pi}_{u,w}=\pi(\mathring{R}_{u,w})
. Since
\pi|_{\mathring{\Sigma}_{w}}
is injective, we get

c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{u,w})=\pi_{*}(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})).

On the other hand, for any
u,w\in W
,

\pi_{*}\big(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})\big)=\pi_{*}\big(c_{\rm SM}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathring{R}_{u,w}})\big)=c_{\rm SM}\big(\pi_{*}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathring{R}_{u,w}})\big).

By Lemma
3.10
, for
u,w\in W
and
v\in W_{P}
,

(11)

\pi_{*}\big(c_{\rm SM}({\mathring{R}_{u,w}})\big)=\pi_{*}\big(c_{\rm SM}({\mathring{R}_{uv,wv}})\big).

By the same reason as in the previous section, we can define the operators
s_{i}^{L}
on
H_{T}^{*}(G/P)
. Moreover, since the projection
\pi
commutes with the
G
-action,
s_{i}^{L}
commutes with the pushforward
\pi_{*}
. Applying
\pi_{*}
to the equation (
10
) in Theorem
4.1
, we get the following Corollary.

Corollary 4.3
.

For
u,w\in W
and
i\in I
, we have

s_{i}^{L}\big(\pi_{*}\big(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})\big)\big)+\alpha_{i}\cdot s_{i}^{L}\big(\pi_{*}\big(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{s_{i}u,w})\big)\big)=\pi_{*}\big(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})\big)+\alpha_{i}\cdot\pi_{*}\big(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,s_{i}w})\big),

where whenever
u\not\leq_{P}w
, the term
c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})
is understood as zero.

Now, let us rewrite the recursion in terms of the extended affine Weyl groups.
Let
\lambda
be a dominant cocharacter whose stabilizer is
W_{P}
. Recall that
f^{\lambda}_{u,w}=ut_{\lambda}w^{-1}
as defined in (
5
). Notice that
f^{\lambda}_{u,w}=f^{\lambda}_{uv,wv}
for any
v\in W_{P}
.
Let us denote

(12)

\mathcal{B}=\{f^{\lambda}_{u,w}\mid u\leq_{P}w\}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{B}^{+}=\{f^{\lambda}_{u,w}\mid u,w\in W\}.

Note that for any
u,w\in W
, there exists a
v\in W_{P}
, such that
wv\in W^{P}
, and
f^{\lambda}_{u,w}=f^{\lambda}_{uv,wv}
. Hence,
\mathcal{B}^{+}=\{f^{\lambda}_{u,w}\mid u\in W,w\in W^{P}\}
.

We parameterize open projected Richardson varieties using
\mathcal{B}^{+}
by denoting

\mathring{\Pi}_{f}=\mathring{\Pi}_{u,w},\qquad\text{ where }f:=f^{\lambda}_{u,w}\text{ for }u\in W,w\in W^{P}.

Note that when
f\notin\mathcal{B}
,
\mathring{\Pi}_{f}=\varnothing
by Proposition
3.5
.
Thanks to (
11
),

\pi_{*}\big(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})\big)=c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f}),\qquad\text{ where }f:=f^{\lambda}_{u,w}\text{ for }u,w\in W..

Hence, Corollary
4.3
can be written as follows.

Corollary 4.4
.

For
f=f^{\lambda}_{u,w}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}
and
i\in I
, we have

(13)

\displaystyle s_{i}^{L}(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f}))+\alpha_{i}\cdot s_{i}^{L}(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{s_{i}f}))=c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})+\alpha_{i}\cdot c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{fs_{i}}).

The torus fixed points
(G/P)^{T}
are indexed by
W/W_{P}\simeq W\lambda
, the Weyl group orbit of
\lambda
.
For any
\mu\in W\lambda
, let

\cdot|_{\mu}:H_{T}^{*}(G/P)\longrightarrow H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt})

denote the localization to the fixed point
\mu
.
By the localization theorem,

H_{T}^{*}(G/P)\longrightarrow H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt})^{\bigoplus W\lambda},\qquad\gamma\longrightarrow(\gamma|_{\mu})_{\mu\in W\lambda}

is an injective map. From the definition of the operator
s_{i}^{L}
, we get for any
\gamma\in H_{T}^{*}(G/P)
,

s_{i}^{L}(\gamma)|_{\mu}=s_{i}(\gamma|_{s_{i}\mu}),

where the right-hand side denotes the usual Weyl group action on
H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt})=\operatorname{Sym}\mathfrak{t}^{*}
.

4.3.
Characterization of Chern classes

First of all, we have the following recursion for the CSM classes of open projected Richardson varieties.

Theorem 4.5
.

Assume we are given

\{\gamma_{f,\mu}\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt}):f\in\mathcal{B}^{+},\mu\in W\lambda\}

such that

\displaystyle\gamma_{ut_{\lambda},\mu}=\delta_{u,\operatorname{id}}\delta_{\mu,\lambda}\prod\nolimits_{\alpha\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+}}(-\alpha),\qquad\forall u\in W,\mu\in W\lambda.

\displaystyle s_{i}(\gamma_{f,s_{i}\mu})+\alpha_{i}\cdot s_{i}(\gamma_{s_{i}f,s_{i}\mu})=\gamma_{f,\mu}+\alpha_{i}\cdot\gamma_{fs_{i},\mu},\qquad\forall f\in\mathcal{B}^{+},\mu\in W\lambda,i\in I.

Then for any
f\in\mathcal{B}^{+}
and
\mu\in W\lambda
,

\gamma_{f,\mu}=c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{\mu}.

Proof.

Let us define

\{\gamma_{u,w,\mu}:u,w\in W,\mu\in W\lambda\}

by setting
\gamma_{u,w,\mu}=\gamma_{f,\mu}
where
f=f^{\lambda}_{u,w}
.
We will show by induction on
w\in W
that

(14)

\gamma_{u,w,\mu}=\pi_{*}\big(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})\big)|_{\mu},\qquad\forall u\in W,\mu\in W\lambda.

If
w=\operatorname{id}
, then
\mathring{R}_{u,w}=\Sigma_{\operatorname{id}}
if
u=\operatorname{id}
, and empty otherwise. Thus,

\pi_{*}\big(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{R}_{u,w})\big)|_{\mu}=\delta_{u,\operatorname{id}}[\Sigma_{\operatorname{id}}]|_{\mu}=\delta_{u,\operatorname{id}}\delta_{\mu,\lambda}\prod\nolimits_{\alpha\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+}}(-\alpha)=\gamma_{u,\operatorname{id},\mu}.

Localizing both sides of (
13
) in Corollary
4.4
to the fixed point
\mu\in G/P
, we get

s_{i}(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{s_{i}\mu})+\alpha_{i}\cdot s_{i}(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{s_{i}f})|_{s_{i}\mu})=c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{\mu}+\alpha_{i}\cdot c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{fs_{i}})|_{\mu}.

Assume (
14
) holds for
w
, then the above equation and the second equation in the Theorem imply that (
14
) also holds for
s_{i}w
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Recall that the SM classes are defined as

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})=\frac{c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})}{c^{T}(T(G/P))}.

We get the following recursion for the SM classes.

Corollary 4.6
.

Assume we are given

\{\gamma_{f,\mu}\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt})_{\operatorname{loc}}:f\in\mathcal{B}^{+},\mu\in W\lambda\}

such that

(15)

\displaystyle\gamma_{ut_{\lambda},\mu}=\delta_{u,\operatorname{id}}\delta_{\mu,\lambda}\prod\nolimits_{\alpha\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+}}\frac{-\alpha}{1-\alpha},\qquad\forall u\in W,\mu\in W\lambda.

(16)

\displaystyle s_{i}(\gamma_{f,s_{i}\mu})+\alpha_{i}\cdot s_{i}(\gamma_{s_{i}f,s_{i}\mu})=\gamma_{f,\mu}+\alpha_{i}\cdot\gamma_{fs_{i},\mu},\qquad\forall u,w\in W,\mu\in W\lambda.

Then for any
f\in\mathcal{B}^{+}
and
\mu\in W\lambda
, we have

\gamma_{f,\mu}=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{\mu}.

Proof.

Since the tangent bundle
T(G/P)
is
G
-equivariant, it is fixed by the action
s_{i}^{L}
. Hence,

s_{i}^{L}(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f}))|_{\mu}=s_{i}(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f}))|_{s_{i}\mu})=\frac{s_{i}(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{s_{i}\mu})}{c^{T}(T(G/P))|_{\mu}}.

Then the Corollary follows from this and Theorem
4.5
.
∎

5.
Recursion of Chern classes, affine part

5.1.
CSM/SM classes of affine flag variety

Let us first recall some properties of the CSM/SM classes of the Schubert cells in the affine flag variety
\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
.

Let
I_{\operatorname{aff}}:=I\sqcup\{0\}
be the vertices of the affine Dynkin diagram. By
[
23
]
,
\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
can be realized as a Kac–Moody flag variety
\hat{G}/\hat{B}
, which is an ind-finite ind-scheme with a stratification by the finite-dimensional Schubert cells. The Weyl group of the Kac–Moody group
\hat{G}
is
\widehat{W}
. Hence, the operator
T_{i}^{L}
for
i\in I_{\operatorname{aff}}
in (
8
) can also be constructed for
H_{*}^{T}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
, with
\alpha_{0}=-\theta

as we only consider the small torus instead of the affine torus in
\hat{G}
, where
\theta
is the highest root.
On the other hand, there is another Weyl group
\widehat{W}
action on
H_{*}^{T}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
, see
[
22
, Definition 5.8]
. To distinguish the operator
w^{L}
from Section
4.1
, we use
w^{R}
to denote this action. For any
i\in I_{\operatorname{aff}}
, we use
\partial_{i}
to denote the BGG operator
[
3
]
, and let

T_{i}^{R}:=\partial_{i}-s_{i}^{R}

be the Hecke operator, see
[
1
,
33
]
. Then both sets of operators
\{T_{i}^{L}\mid i\in I_{\operatorname{aff}}\}
and
\{T_{i}^{R}\mid i\in I_{\operatorname{aff}}\}
satisfy the relations in
\widehat{W}
, and
T_{i}^{R}T_{j}^{L}=T_{j}^{L}T_{i}^{R}
.

Recall that for any
f\in\widehat{W}
,
\mathring{\Sigma}_{f}
denotes the Schubert cell. The CSM class
c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{f})\in H_{*}^{T}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
is well defined as
\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
is an ind-scheme, and they form a basis for the localized equivariant homology group. Since the Schubert variety
\Sigma_{f}:=\overline{\mathring{\Sigma}_{f}}
also has a Bott–Samelson resolution as in the finite type case (see
[
23
]
), the proof of
[
1
, Theorem 1.1]
also works for the affine flag variety
\operatorname{Fl}_{G}
. Hence, we have the following formula

T_{i}^{R}(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{f}))=c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{fs_{i}}),

where
i\in I_{\operatorname{aff}}
. Moreover, the proof in
[
33
, Theorem 4.3]
only depends on the above formula and a computation for
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we get

T_{i}^{L}(c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{f}))=c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{s_{i}f}).

Recall that the
T
-equivariant cohomology of the affine flag variety
H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
can be identified with
\operatorname{Hom}_{H^{*}_{T}(\operatorname{pt})}(H^{T}_{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G}),H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt}))
, see
[
27
, Chapter 4]
. Hence, there is a perfect pairing

\langle-,-\rangle:H^{T}_{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})\times H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})\rightarrow H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt}).

We define the
SM class of opposite Schubert cells

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
to be the dual basis of the CSM classes:

(17)

\langle c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{f}),s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{g})\rangle=\delta_{f,g}.

Remark 5.1
.

To be more precise, we need to consider the thick affine flag variety
\tilde{\operatorname{Fl}}_{G}
, and the SM class is defined in the cohomology ring
H_{T}^{*}(\tilde{\operatorname{Fl}}_{G})
, which is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Hom}_{H^{*}_{T}(\operatorname{pt})}(H^{T}_{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G}),H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt}))
, see
[
27
, Proposition 3.46 in Chapter 4]
.

Let

T_{i}^{L,\vee}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}\operatorname{id}+\frac{\alpha_{i}-1}{\alpha_{i}}s_{i}^{L},\quad\text{and}\quad T_{i}^{R,\vee}:=\partial_{i}+s_{i}^{R}.

Then for any
\gamma_{1}\in H_{*}^{T}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
and
\gamma_{2}\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
, we have (see
[
2
,
33
]
)

\langle T_{i}^{R}(\gamma_{1}),\gamma_{2}\rangle=\langle\gamma_{1},T_{i}^{R,\vee}(\gamma_{2})\rangle,\text{\quad and \quad}\langle T_{i}^{L}(\gamma_{1}),\gamma_{2}\rangle=s_{i}\cdot\langle\gamma_{1},T_{i}^{L,\vee}(\gamma_{2})\rangle.

Combining all the above equations, we get

T_{i}^{L,\vee}(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f}))=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{s_{i}f}),\qquad T_{i}^{R,\vee}(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f}))=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{fs_{i}}).

For any
f\in\widehat{W}
, we use
[e_{f}]\in H_{*}^{T}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
to denote the class of the fixed point corresponding to
f
. Then we have the following formula (see
[
33
]
):

T_{i}^{R}([e_{f}])=\frac{1+f\alpha_{i}}{f\alpha_{i}}[e_{fs_{i}}]-\frac{1}{f\alpha_{i}}[e_{f}].

5.2.
Recursions of affine SM classes

Recall that
H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
is defined to be the dual of
H_{*}^{T}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
, there is a well-defined localization at fixed points as follows (see
[
27
, Chapter 4]
)

\gamma|_{f}:=\langle[e_{f}],\gamma\rangle,

where
f\in\widehat{W}
and
\gamma\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
.
Then we have the following recursion for the localization of the SM classes. Recall that
\alpha_{0}=-\theta
with
\theta
being the highest root.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
f,g\in\widehat{W}
.
For any
i\in I_{\operatorname{aff}}
, we have

(18)

\displaystyle(\alpha_{i}+1)s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{s_{i}g}

\displaystyle=s_{i}\big(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{g}\big)+\alpha_{i}\cdot s_{i}\big(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{s_{i}f})|_{g}\big),

(19)

\displaystyle(g(\alpha_{i})+1)s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{gs_{i}}

\displaystyle=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{g}+g(\alpha_{i})\cdot s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{fs_{i}})|_{g}.

Proof.

The first one follows from the definition of
T_{i}^{L,\vee}
,
T_{i}^{L,\vee}(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f}))=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{s_{i}f})
, and the fact that
s_{i}^{L}(\gamma)|_{g}=s_{i}(\gamma|_{s_{i}g})
for any
\gamma\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
. For the second one, we have

\displaystyle s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{fs_{i}})|_{g}

\displaystyle=\langle[e_{g}],s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{fs_{i}})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle[e_{g}],T_{i}^{R,\vee}(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f}))\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle T_{i}^{R}([e_{g}]),s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1+g\alpha_{i}}{g\alpha_{i}}s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{gs_{i}}-\frac{1}{g\alpha_{i}}s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{g}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Recall that the action of
t_{\mu}\in\widehat{W}
on the lattice
X^{*}(T)\oplus\mathbb{Z}\delta
is given by the following formula

t_{\mu}(\lambda+k\delta)=\lambda+(k-\langle\lambda,\mu\rangle)\delta,

where
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
is a character of the maximal torus
T
, and
\delta
is the imaginary root for the corresponding affine Kac–Moody algebra. Since we are considering the small torus
T
,
\delta
is zero in
H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt})
. Hence, for any
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
, and
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
,
t_{\mu}(\lambda)=\lambda
. Therefore,
for any
i\in I
and
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
,
by substituting
g=t_{s_{i}\mu}
in (
18
) and
g=t_{\mu}
in
(
19
), we have

(20)

s_{i}\big(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{t_{s_{i}\mu}}\big)+\alpha_{i}\cdot s_{i}\big(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{s_{i}f})|_{t_{s_{i}\mu}}\big)=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{t_{\mu}}+\alpha_{i}\cdot s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{fs_{i}})|_{t_{\mu}}.

This equality takes the form of (
16
), and will be used in the proof of Theorem
6.3
.

6.
Comparison between the Chern classes

In this section, we will combine the results in the previous two sections to obtain a relationship between the SM classes of open projected Richardson cells and the SM classes of affine Schubert cells.

As before, let
\lambda
be a dominant cocharacter and
P
be a parabolic subgroup containing
B
such that
W_{P}
is the stabilizer of
\lambda
in
W
. Let
f=f^{\lambda}_{u,w}=ut_{\lambda}w^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}
, where
u\in W
and
w\in W^{P}
. Then we have the SM class
s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})\in H_{T}^{*}(G/P)
of the open projected Richardson variety.
On the other hand,
f
can be regarded as an element in the extended affine Weyl group
\widehat{W}
, and we have the SM class
s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
.

For any
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
and
\gamma\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Gr}_{G})
, let
\gamma|_{t_{\mu}W}
be the localization at the fixed point
t_{\mu}W
in
\operatorname{Gr}_{G}
.
There is a pullback map (see
[
27
, Proposition 4.4 in Chapter 4]
)

r^{*}\colon H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})\rightarrow H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Gr}_{G}).

In terms of localization, this is defined as follows

r^{*}(\gamma)|_{t_{\mu}W}=\gamma|_{t_{\mu}},

where
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
.

Recall that
i_{\lambda}:G/P\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}
and
j_{\lambda}:\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{G}
are inclusions.
Let
q_{\lambda}^{*}:H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})\rightarrow H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda})
be the composition of
j_{\lambda}^{*}\circ r^{*}
.
The following is one of the main results of this paper.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be the normal bundle of
G/P
inside
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}
. Then, for any
f=ut_{\lambda}w^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}
,

i_{\lambda,*}\bigg(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})\cdot c^{T}(\mathcal{N})^{-1}\bigg)=q_{\lambda}^{*}\bigg(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})\bigg)\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda})_{\operatorname{loc}},

where
c^{T}(\mathcal{N})
is the
T
-equivariant total Chern class of
\mathcal{N}
.

Remark 6.2
.

By taking the lowest degree terms, we obtain
[
15
, Theorem 5.8]
, the type
A
case was proved in
[
19
, Theorem 7.8]
.

Proof.

By the localization theorem, we only need to check that both sides have the same localizations at the torus fixed points. The torus fixed points
(\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda})^{T}
are
\{t_{\mu}W\mid\mu\in W\lambda\}
. Notice that the
T
-weights of the tangent space of
G/P
at the identity point is
\{-\alpha\mid\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle>0\}
. Hence, by Lemma
2.3
,

c^{T}(\mathcal{N})|_{\lambda}=\prod_{\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle>0}(1-\alpha)^{\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle-1}.

Therefore, by the
G
-equivariance of
\mathcal{N}
,

c^{T}(\mathcal{N})|_{\mu}=\prod_{\langle\mu,\alpha\rangle>0}(1-\alpha)^{\langle\mu,\alpha\rangle-1}

for any
\mu\in W(\lambda)
.
On the other hand, by the equivariant localization theorem,

\displaystyle i_{\lambda,*}\bigg(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})\cdot c^{T}(\mathcal{N})^{-1}\bigg)\bigg|_{t_{\mu}W}

\displaystyle=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{\mu}\cdot\frac{e^{T}(\mathcal{N})|_{\mu}}{c^{T}(\mathcal{N})|_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{\mu}\prod_{\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle>0}\left(\frac{1-\alpha}{-\alpha}\right)^{-\langle\mu,\alpha\rangle+1},

where
e^{T}(\mathcal{N})
denotes the equivariant Euler class of
\mathcal{N}
.
Then the theorem follows from the above equation and Theorem
6.3
below.
∎

Theorem 6.3
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{B}^{+}
and
\mu\in W\lambda
,

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{\mu}=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{t_{\mu}}\prod_{\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle>0}\left(\frac{1-\alpha}{-\alpha}\right)^{\langle\mu,\alpha\rangle-1}\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt})_{\operatorname{loc}},

where the product is over all the roots
\alpha
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\gamma}_{f,\mu}
denote the right-hand side of the equation, then we only need to check that
\tilde{\gamma}_{f,\mu}
satisfies the two equations in Corollary
4.6
. Let us first check (
15
). If

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{ut_{\lambda}})|_{t_{\mu}}\neq 0,

then
ut_{\lambda}\leq t_{\mu}
. By Theorem
3.8
, we must have
u=\operatorname{id}
. But
t_{\lambda}\leq t_{\mu}
if and only if
\lambda=\mu
since
\ell(t_{\lambda})=\ell(t_{\mu})
.
Let us compute
s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{t_{\lambda}})|_{t_{\lambda}}
. Recall that for any
w\in\widehat{W}
,
[e_{w}]\in H_{*}^{T}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
denotes the class of the corresponding fixed point. Since the CSM class
c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{t_{\lambda}})
is supported on the closure
\overline{\mathring{\Sigma}_{t_{\lambda}}}
, we have

c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{t_{\lambda}})=\sum_{g\in\widehat{W},g\leq t_{\lambda}}a_{g}[e_{g}],

for some coefficients
a_{g}\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt})_{\operatorname{loc}}
. Moreover, the leading coefficient
a_{t_{\lambda}}
, which is the equivariant multiplicity of
c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{t_{\lambda}})
at the torus fixed point
t_{\lambda}
, equals

a_{t_{\lambda}}=\prod_{\chi}\frac{\chi+1}{\chi}=\prod_{\alpha>0}\bigg(\frac{1-\alpha}{-\alpha}\bigg)^{\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle},

Here the first equality follows from
[
34
, Theorem 6.5]
with
\chi
being the
T
-weights of the tangent space
T_{t_{\lambda}}(\mathcal{I}t_{\lambda}\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I})
, while the last one follows from Lemma
2.2
. Therefore,

[e_{t_{\lambda}}]=\sum_{g\in\widehat{W},g\leq t_{\lambda}}b_{g}c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}_{g}),

for some coefficient
b_{g}\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt})_{\operatorname{loc}}
, with

b_{t_{\lambda}}=\frac{1}{a_{t_{\lambda}}}=\prod_{\alpha>0}\bigg(\frac{-\alpha}{1-\alpha}\bigg)^{\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle}.

Therefore, by the definition of SM class in (
17
),

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{t_{\lambda}})|_{t_{\lambda}}=\langle[e_{t_{\lambda}}],s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{t_{\lambda}})\rangle=b_{t_{\lambda}}=\prod_{\alpha>0}\bigg(\frac{-\alpha}{1-\alpha}\bigg)^{\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle}.

Hence, (
15
) is satisfied.

Finally, (
16
) follows from (
20
) and the fact that

\prod_{\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle>0}\left(\frac{1-\alpha}{-\alpha}\right)^{\langle\mu,\alpha\rangle-1}=s_{i}\bigg(\prod_{\langle\alpha,s_{i}\mu\rangle>0}\left(\frac{1-\alpha}{-\alpha}\right)^{\langle s_{i}\mu,\alpha\rangle-1}\bigg).\qed

Example 6.4
.

When
\lambda
is minuscule,
G/P
is said to be cominuscule.
In this case
\operatorname{Gr}_{\lambda}\simeq G/P
and
\mathcal{N}
is trivial, see
[
44
]
.
We have

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})=q^{*}_{\lambda}\bigg(s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})\bigg).

Example 6.5
.

When
u=w\in W^{P}
,
the corresponding
f^{\lambda}_{u,w}=t_{\mu}
for
\mu=w\lambda
.
In this case the cell
\mathring{\Pi}_{f}
is the fixed point corresponding to
w
. In particular,

c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{\mu}=\delta_{\mu\lambda}\prod_{\beta\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+}}(-w\beta)=\delta_{\mu\lambda}\prod_{\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle>0}(-\alpha).

As a result,

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{\mu}=\delta_{\mu\lambda}\prod_{\beta\in R^{+}\setminus R_{P}^{+}}(-w\beta)=\delta_{\mu\lambda}\prod_{\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle>0}\frac{-\alpha}{1-\alpha}.

On the other hand,

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{t_{\mu}}=\delta_{\mu\lambda}\prod_{\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle>0}\left(\frac{-\alpha}{1-\alpha}\right)^{\langle\mu,\alpha\rangle}.

This verifies Theorem
6.3
when
f=t_{\mu}
.

7.
Combinatorial formula in type
A

In this section, we aim to provide a combinatorial formula for
s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})
in the case of type
A
via a string diagram inside
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
In the type
A
case, one can naturally identify

X_{*}(T)=\mathbb{Z}\mathbf{e}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}\mathbf{e}_{n}\supset\mathbb{Z}(\mathbf{e}_{1}-\mathbf{e}_{2})\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Z}(\mathbf{e}_{n-1}-\mathbf{e}_{n})=Q^{\vee}.

The extended affine Weyl group
\widehat{W}
can be realized as the group of
n
-periodic affine permutations

\tilde{S}_{n}=\left\{\text{bijections $f\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}$ such that
$f(i+n)=f(i)+n$}\right\}.

For a cocharacter
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in X_{*}(T)
and
w\in S_{n}
,
wt_{\lambda}\in\tilde{S}_{n}
is the affine permutation determined by

wt_{\lambda}(i)=w(i)+\lambda_{i}\cdot n,\qquad 1\leq i\leq n.

To derive our combinatorial formula for
s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})
, we need a localization formula for
s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})
as given in Theorem
7.3
.

7.1.
Localization formula for
s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})

Following the classical convention of Schubert calculus, we identify

H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt})=\mathbb{Q}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}]
where

y_{i}\in X^{*}(T)
such that
\langle y_{i},\mathbf{e}_{j}\rangle=-\delta_{ij}
.
In particular, we have

\alpha_{i}=-y_{i}+y_{i+1}\quad(1\leq i\leq n-1),\quad\alpha_{0}=-y_{n}+y_{1}.

We shall represent any element
g\in\tilde{S}_{n}
by an
n
-periodic string diagram
\mathcal{D}_{g}
.
Intuitively, an
n
-periodic string diagram is a diagram
\mathcal{D}
of upward strings inside
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, whose endpoints are the lattice points
(i,0)

and
(i,1)
with
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
such that it satisfies

\mathcal{D}+(n,0)=\mathcal{D}
as well as the following conditions:

(1)

all strings are smooth with tangent direction in
[0^{\circ},180^{\circ})
at each point;

(2)

the intersection of any three strings must be empty;

(3)

the tangent direction at the intersection point
of two strings
are different.

That is, the following configurations of strings are banned

\begin{matrix}\hbox to40.63pt{\vbox to40.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\mathbf{(X)}\end{matrix}

For
g\in\tilde{S}_{n}
, a diagram
\mathcal{D}_{g}
is obtained by drawing a string connecting the endpoints
(g^{-1}(i),0)
and
(i,1)
for each
1\leq i\leq n
, and translating this local configuration of
n
strings horizontally such that the resulting diagram is
n
-periodic. Note that such diagrams are not unique. However, as will be seen later, we shall concern the weight generating function of
\mathcal{D}_{g}
, which is independent of the choice of
\mathcal{D}_{g}
.
In the following example, we illustrate a string diagram

for the affine permutation
g\in\tilde{S}_{5}
with

g(1)=4,\quad g(2)=1,\quad g(3)=7,\quad g(4)=3,\quad g(5)=5.

To state the localization formula, we define two colorings on a string diagram
\mathcal{D}
.
An
n
-periodic coloring of
endpoints
of
\mathcal{D}
is a map
\beta\colon P\mapsto\beta(P)\in\mathbb{Z}
, which assigns each endpoint
P
with an integer such that
\beta(P+(n,0))=\beta(P)+n
.
Notice that the intersection points of strings cut the strings into pieces. An
n
-periodic coloring
\kappa
on the
pieces
assigns each piece an integer such that

•

for any piece
p
,
\kappa(p+(n,0))=\kappa(p)+n
;

•

if an intersection point
P
of two strings has surrounding pieces colored as follows

then we require that either
a=x\neq b=y
or
a=y\neq x=b
.

We say that a coloring
\kappa
of pieces is
compatible
with a coloring
\beta
of endpoints if for any piece
p
and each endpoint
P
of
p
, we have
\kappa(p)=\beta(P)
.

we see that
\kappa
is compatible with
\beta
, where we set
\beta(i,0)=i
and

\beta(1,1)=1,\quad\beta(2,1)=0,\quad\beta(3,1)=3,\quad\beta(4,1)=2,\quad\beta(5,1)=4,\quad\text{etc}.

In the remaining of this section, we always assume that
\kappa
is compatible with
\beta
.

By assigning a weight to each string (not each piece), we can define the weight of each intersection point
P
in the following manner:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{matrix}\right)=\frac{1}{1+u-v}\begin{cases}1,&a=x\neq b=y,\\
u-v,&a=y\neq x=b.\end{cases}

Here
u,v
are the weights of two intersecting strings.
The weight
\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D},\beta,\kappa)
of a string diagram
\mathcal{D}
is defined as the product of weights of all intersection points inside one periodicity
(\epsilon,\epsilon+n]\times\mathbb{R}
for any generic
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover, we define the weight
\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D},\beta)
to be the sum of

\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D},\beta,\kappa)
with
\kappa
running through colorings compatible with
\beta
.

The following property is well known, see for example
[
21
, Proposition 2.1]
.

Theorem 7.1
.

\text{normalization }{\mathbf{(Nm)}}\end{matrix}

Remark 7.2
.

Notice that the
\mathbf{(YBE)}
could be viewed as a version of braid relation in type
A
.

For
g\in\tilde{S}_{n}
, the first two equations in Theorem
7.1
imply that

\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\beta)
is well defined, that is, it is independent of the choice of the string diagram
\mathcal{D}_{g}
.
In fact, since the configuration in
\mathbf{(S)}
is not allowed, one can deform any string, with two endpoints fixed, via moving horizontally.
If we choose the deformation generically, then, during the movement,
we will only meet the local configurations
\mathbf{(M)}
and
\mathbf{(X)}
.
So the string diagrams just before and just after have the same weights by
\mathbf{(YBE)}
and
\mathbf{(UE)}
respectively. This means that
\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\beta)
only depends on how the endpoints are connected.

We now assign the string connecting
(g^{-1}(i),0)

and
(i,1)
with the weight
y_{i}
, where
1\leq i\leq n
.
For
f\in\tilde{S}_{n}
, we define
\beta_{f}
as the coloring such that
\beta_{f}(i,0)=i
and
\beta_{f}(i,1)=f^{-1}(i)
.
Diagrammatically, it looks like

The following localization formula should be known to experts. We include a brief argument here since we could not find a proof in the literature.

Theorem 7.3
.

For
f,g\in\tilde{S}_{n}
, we have

\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\beta_{f})=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{g}.

Proof.

When
\ell(g)=0
, it is easily checked that

\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\beta_{f})=\delta_{f,g}=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Sigma}^{f})|_{g}.

For any
g
and
i\in I\cup\{0\}
, consider the following string diagram for
s_{i}g
:

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{matrix}\qquad\beta_{j}=f^{-1}(j)

Removing the intersection point gives a string diagram
\mathcal{D}_{g}
. Notice that there are two choices for the colors of the lower two pieces attached to this intersection point. This yields the following equality

\displaystyle\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D}_{s_{i}g},\beta_{f})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+y_{i+1}-y_{i}}\left(s_{i}\big(\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\beta_{f})\big)+(y_{i+1}-y_{i})s_{i}\big(\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\beta_{s_{i}f})\big)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{i}}\left(s_{i}\big(\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\beta_{f})\big)+\alpha_{i}s_{i}\big(\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D}_{g},\beta_{s_{i}f})\big)\right),

which agrees with the recurrence (
18
) in Proposition
5.2
.
So the theorem follows by induction.
∎

7.2.
Positroid varieties

We are now in a position to construct the symmetric rational function, denoted
\tilde{F}_{f}
, which represents the class
s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})
of the open positroid variety
\mathring{\Pi}_{f}
in the Grassmannian.
We choose the minuscule cocharacter

\lambda=\mathbf{e}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{e}_{k}\in X_{*}(T).

Then
G/P
is the Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
.
In this case, open projected Richardson varieties are known as open positroid varieties, which are indexed by bounded affine permutations
[
19
]
:

\mathcal{B}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\text{bijections $f\colon\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}$}\end{array}:\begin{array}[]{c}f(i+n)=f(i)+n\\[4.30554pt]
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f(i)-i)=k\\[4.30554pt]
i\leq f(i)\leq i+n\end{array}\right\}.

Combining Example
6.4
and Theorem
7.3
, we obtain the following localization formula for
s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})
.

Corollary 7.4
.

We have

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{\lambda}=\operatorname{wt}(\mathcal{D}_{t_{\lambda}},\beta_{f})
.

Remark 7.5
.

It could be possible to compute the left-hand side by finding explicit resolutions, similar to the approaches of
[
24
, Proposition 6.2, Corollary 6.3]
.

To define
\tilde{F}_{f}
, let us consider another type of string diagrams: the grid
\Delta
in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
including
k
horizontal lines. Color the endpoints of vertical lines using
\beta_{f}
as in Subsection
7.1
. The horizontal and vertical lines are assigned with weights
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
as illustrated below:

Similarly, we may define
n
-periodic colorings
\kappa
of the segments connecting the intersection points. For each
\kappa
which is compatible with
\beta_{f}
, we accordingly define the weight
\operatorname{wt}(\Delta,\beta_{f},\kappa)
as the product of weights of all intersection points inside one periodicity. Here the weight of an intersection point obeys the same rule as defined in
Subsection
7.1
.
Summing over all colorings
\kappa
compatible with
\beta_{f}
, we obtain
the weight generating function
\operatorname{wt}(\Delta,\beta_{f})
, which is the function that we require:

\tilde{F}_{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k};y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}):=\operatorname{wt}(\Delta,\beta_{f}).

Theorem 7.6
.

For
f\in\mathcal{B}
, the function
\tilde{F}_{f}
is symmetric in
x
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\tilde{F}_{f}
is symmetric if exchanging
x_{i}
and
x_{i+1}
.
This is illustrated through the following diagrammatic calculation:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\hbox to120.3pt{\vbox to42.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-11.91324pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={3.0pt,3.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=1.2pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,1,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 151.57 41.34 L 151.57 -13.78}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 165.35 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

Theorem 7.7
.

For
f\in\mathcal{B}
, we have

\tilde{F}_{f}=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{C}^{n}))_{\operatorname{loc}}.

Proof.

It is enough to check that

\tilde{F}_{f}|_{\mu}=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{\mu}

for any
\mu\in S_{n}\lambda
.
Let
a_{1}<\cdots<a_{k}
be the indices such that
\mu_{a_{i}}=1
. Then

\tilde{F}_{f}|_{\mu}=\tilde{F}_{f}(y_{a_{1}},\ldots,y_{a_{k}};y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{pt}).

We see that (
a=a_{i}
in the following diagram)

So, after the specialization
x_{i}\mapsto y_{a_{i}}
, the diagram
\Delta
becomes a string diagram for
t_{\mu}
.
By Corollary
7.4
, we have
\tilde{F}_{f}|_{\mu}=s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})|_{\mu}
.
∎

For a concrete example to illustrate the above proof, consider the case
(k,n)=(3,7)
and
\mu=(1,0,1,0,0,1,0)
. Then
t_{\mu}(1)=8,t_{\mu}(2)=2,t_{\mu}(3)=10,t_{\mu}(4)=4,t_{\mu}(5)=5,t_{\mu}(6)=13,t_{\mu}(7)=7.
By
(Nm)
, we have

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{matrix}=\mathcal{D}_{t_{\mu}}.

7.3.
Pipe dream model

If we take the Poincaré dual of the diagram above, we will reach a pipe dream model for
\tilde{F}_{f}
as follows.
Consider all possible
n
-periodic tiling on the square grid
\{1,\ldots,k\}\times\mathbb{Z}
using tiles

\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{1}{0.5}{0.5}{0}{0.5}\end{picture}\end{array}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\end{picture}\end{array}}\end{array}

Here, as usual,
n
-periodicity means the tile at
(i,j)
is the same as that at
(i+n,j)
. We emphasize that
i
denotes the row index, counted from top to bottom, and
j
denotes the column index, counted from left to right.
Let us denote by
\mathsf{PD}(f)
the set of all such tilings with reading affine permutation
f
.
Alternatively, each such tiling is obtained from a triple
(\Delta,\beta_{f},\kappa)
by reconnecting the edges around each vertex such that two edges are joined if they receive the same color from
\kappa
.
For example, when
n=7
,
k=3
,
the following tiling

\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\qbezier{}{0}{0}{0}{0.1}{0}{0.1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.4}{0}{0.6}{0}{0.6}\qbezier{}{0}{0.9}{0}{1}{0}{1}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$\text{-}6$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$\text{-}5$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$\text{-}4$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$\text{-}3$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$\text{-}2$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$\text{-}1$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$0$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\qbezier{}{0}{0}{0}{0.1}{0}{0.1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.4}{0}{0.6}{0}{0.6}\qbezier{}{0}{0.9}{0}{1}{0}{1}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$1$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$2$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$3$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$4$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$5$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$6$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$7$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\qbezier{}{0}{0}{0}{0.1}{0}{0.1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.4}{0}{0.6}{0}{0.6}\qbezier{}{0}{0.9}{0}{1}{0}{1}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$8$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$9$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$1\kern-1.00006pt0$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$1\kern-1.00006pt1$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$1\kern-1.00006pt2$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$1\kern-1.00006pt3$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$1\kern-1.00006pt4$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\qbezier{}{0}{0}{0}{0.1}{0}{0.1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.4}{0}{0.6}{0}{0.6}\qbezier{}{0}{0.9}{0}{1}{0}{1}\end{picture}\\
\end{array}}\end{array}

is a pipe dream with reading affine permutation given by

f(1)=2,\,\,f(2)=6,\,\,f(3)=5,\,\,f(4)=10,\,\,f(5)=8,\,\,f(6)=11,\,\,f(7)=7.

For
\pi\in\mathsf{PD}(f)
, set

\operatorname{wt}(\pi)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1+x_{i}-y_{j}}\begin{cases}1,&\text{the $(i,j)$-position is $\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{1}{0.5}{0.5}{0}{0.5}\end{picture}\end{array}}\end{array}$},\\
x_{i}-y_{j},&\text{the $(i,j)$-position is $\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\end{picture}\end{array}}\end{array}$}.\end{cases}

Then, in the language of pipe dreams, we see that

\tilde{F}_{f}=\sum_{\pi\in\mathsf{PD}(f)}\operatorname{wt}(\pi).

Remark 7.8
.

When compared with
[
42
]
, it follows that
the lowest degree component of
\tilde{F}_{f}
is the double affine Stanley symmetric function
[
28
]
.

Example 7.9
.

Consider the case
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\cong\operatorname{Gr}(1,n)
.
Let
f\in\mathcal{B}
. Define

A=\{1\leq i\leq n:f(i)=i\}\subset[n].

Note that there is only one element in
\mathsf{PD}(f)
, that is, for
1\leq i\leq n
,

\text{the $(1,i)$ tile is }\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{1}{0.5}{0.5}{0}{0.5}\end{picture}\end{array}}\end{array},&i\notin A,\\
\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\end{picture}\end{array}}\end{array},&i\in A.\end{cases}

For example, when
A=\{2,4,5,6,9\}\subset[9]
,

\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\qbezier{}{0}{0}{0}{0.1}{0}{0.1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.4}{0}{0.6}{0}{0.6}\qbezier{}{0}{0.9}{0}{1}{0}{1}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$\text{-}1$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$2$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$1$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$4$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$5$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$6$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$3$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$7$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$9$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\qbezier{}{0}{0}{0}{0.1}{0}{0.1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.4}{0}{0.6}{0}{0.6}\qbezier{}{0}{0.9}{0}{1}{0}{1}\end{picture}\\
\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\qbezier{}{0}{0}{0}{0.1}{0}{0.1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.4}{0}{0.6}{0}{0.6}\qbezier{}{0}{0.9}{0}{1}{0}{1}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{1}{0.5}{0.5}{0}{0.5}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{1}{0.5}{0.5}{0}{0.5}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{0.5}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,1.0){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\qbezier{}{0.5}{0}{0.5}{0.5}{1}{ \begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\qbezier{}{0}{0}{0}{0.1}{0}{0.1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.4}{0}{0.6}{0}{0.6}\qbezier{}{0}{0.9}{0}{1}{0}{1}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$1$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$2$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$3$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$4$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$5$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$6$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$7$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$8$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.2){\makebox[12.0pt]{$9$}}\end{picture}\begin{picture}\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\qbezier{}{0}{0}{0}{0.1}{0}{0.1}\qbezier{}{0}{0.4}{0}{0.6}{0}{0.6}\qbezier{}{0}{0.9}{0}{1}{0}{1}\end{picture}\\
\end{array}}\end{array}

is the only element in
\mathsf{PD}(f)
with weight

\frac{(x-y_{2})(x-y_{4})(x-y_{5})(x-y_{6})(x-y_{9})}{(1+x-y_{1})(1+x-y_{2})\cdots(1+x-y_{9})}.

Geometrically, the open positroid variety can be described as a torus orbit

\mathring{\Pi}_{f}=\big\{[x_{1}:\cdots:x_{n}]:x_{i}=i\iff i\in A\big\}.

Note that in a smooth toric variety, the CSM class of a toric orbit is nothing but the fundamental class of its closure
[
10
, Section 5.3 Lemma]
, so

c_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})=[\Pi_{f}]=\prod_{i\in A}(x-y_{i})\in H_{T}^{*}(\mathbb{P}^{n-1}).

Thus

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1+x-y_{i}}\begin{cases}1,&i\notin A,\\
x-y_{i},&i\in A.\end{cases}

This agrees with our formula.

Example 7.10
.

Let
f\in\tilde{S}_{4}
be such that

f(1)=2,\quad f(2)=5,\quad f(3)=4,\quad f(4)=7

Compute
s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})\in H_{T}^{*}(\operatorname{Gr}(2,4))
. There are six elements in
\mathsf{PD}(f)
:

\end{array}}\end{array}

Thus

s_{\rm SM}(\mathring{\Pi}_{f})=\frac{1}{\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{2}\prod_{j=1}^{4}(1+x_{i}-y_{j})}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(x_{2}-y_{1})(x_{2}-y_{3})+(x_{1}-y_{2})(x_{2}-y_{3})\\
+(x_{1}-y_{4})(x_{2}-y_{1})+(x_{1}-y_{2})(x_{1}-y_{4})\\
+(x_{1}-y_{3})(x_{1}-y_{4})(x_{2}-y_{1})(x_{2}-y_{2})\\
+(x_{1}-y_{1})(x_{1}-y_{2})(x_{2}-y_{3})(x_{2}-y_{4})\end{array}\right).
